Mesoscopic Edge Universality of Orthogonal Polynomial Ensembles
Wenkui Liu

Abstract
In this paper, we study the mesoscopic fluctuations at edges of orthogonal polynomial ensembles with both continuous and discrete measures. Our main result is a Central Limit Theorem (CLT) for linear statistics at mesoscopic scales. We show that if the recurrence coefficients for the associated orthogonal polynomials are slowly varying, a universal CLT holds. Our primary tool is the resolvent for the truncated Jacobi matrices associated with the orthogonal polynomials. While the Combes-Thomas estimate has been successful in obtaining bulk mesoscopic fluctuations in the literature, it is too rough at the edges. Instead, we prove an estimate for the resolvent of Jacobi matrices with slowly varying entries. Particular examples to which our CLT applies are Jacobi, Laguerre and Gaussian unitary ensembles, as well as discrete ensembles from random tilings.



MSC2020: 60B20, 60F05

1
Introduction

Let
\mu
be a Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}
with finite moments, i.e., for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{k}d\mu(x)<\infty
. The orthogonal polynomial ensemble (OPE) of order
n\in\mathbb{N}
associated to
\mu
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
proportional to

\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}d\mu(x_{1})\cdots d\mu(x_{n}).

(1)

In this paper, we will be interested in studying the behaviour of this ensemble for large
n
.

OPEs arise naturally in many models of statistical mechanics, probability theory, combinatorics, and random matrix theory. For some surveys, we refer to
[
5
,
22
,
24
,
26
]
. An important and well-known source of examples where OPEs appear is the eigenvalues of random Hermitian matrices, where the probability measure is invariant under the conjugation with unitary matrices. classical examples are the Jacobi, Laguerre and Gaussian Unitary Ensembles. In these cases, the measure
\mu
is absolutely continuous with an analytic density. The support of the measure can be compact, for example, the (modified) Jacobi Unitary Ensemble, or unbounded, for example the Gaussian and Laguerre Unitary Ensembles. When the support of the measure is unbounded, it is natural to rescale the measure by
n
, so that the eigenvalues accumulate on finitely many intervals with high probability as
n\to\infty
. For this reason, we will allow the measure
\mu
to depend on
n
and write
\mu=\mu_{n}
.

OPEs with discrete measures are also natural objects to be considered. For instance, uniformly distributed random lozenge tilings of a hexagon give rise to (extended) OPEs associated with the Hahn measure, see
[
24
]
. Similarly, uniformly distributed random domino tilings of an Aztec diamond give rise to (extended) OPEs associated with the Krawtchouk measure, see
[
23
]
. In such cases, the measure
\mu
is discrete and its parameters depend on the size of the tiling, i.e.,
\mu=\mu_{n}
. This is another reason that we allow the measure to be
n
-dependent.

We will study the asymptotic behaviour as
n\to\infty
and explore cases of
\mu
(or
\mu_{n}
) both continuous and discrete in this paper. There are three regimes that one can look into, i.e., macroscopic, mesoscopic and microscopic regimes. In the macroscopic regime, one studies the global behaviour of the point processes, whereas in the microscopic regime, one zooms in near a point and studies the nearest neighbour interactions between points. This paper studies the mesoscopic regime which is intermediate between the other two and we will focus on the edges.

Let us, for clarity, start with the example of the Laguerre Unitary Ensemble, whose measure is given by
d\mu_{n}(x)=x^{\gamma}e^{-nx}dx
on
[0,+\infty)
, for some parameter
\gamma>-1
. It describes the eigenvalue distribution of a Wishart matrix. It is a classical example that has both a hard and soft edge and will, therefore, be an interesting example for the techniques developed in this paper. For the Laguerre Unitary Ensemble, the eigenvalues accumulate on the interval
[0,4]
almost surely as
n\to\infty
and have the limiting distribution
d\rho_{\mu}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{4-x}{x}}dx
, see Figure
1
. In the bulk, i.e., any point in the interval
(0,4)
, the typical distance between neighbouring points is of order
\sim n^{-1}
. Near the edges, the scaling is different. The origin is a hard edge of the interval, since no eigenvalues can be negative. Near the origin, the limiting density behaves as
d\rho_{\mu}(x)\sim 1/\sqrt{x}dx
, which is typical at hard edges, and the neighbouring points are of distance
\sim n^{-2}
. The right-end point of the interval is a typical example of soft edge where the density vanishes as square root
d\rho_{\mu}(x)\sim\sqrt{4-x}dx
and the distance between neighbours is
\sim n^{-\frac{2}{3}}
. It is well-known that the microscopic process of this Laguerre Unitary Ensemble converges to the Bessel point process at the hard edge, sine point process in bulk and Airy point process at the soft edge. These scaling limits on the microscopic scale are universal and observed in a wide variety of models, see
[
15
,
11
,
34
,
40
]
. We also point out that
[
30
]
offers an overview of universality.

4
x
\qquad\qquad d\rho_{\mu}(x)/dx

Figure 1
:
The equilibrium measure of Laguerre Unitary Ensemble. Hard edge
x=0
, soft edge
x=4
.

We will study the mesoscopic scales at edges using the scaled linear statistics of the following form
X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}
, where the test function
f
is a compactly supported real-valued function, the location is
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, and the scale is
n^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha>0
,

X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(n^{\alpha}(x_{i}-x_{0})).

(2)

These scaled linear statistics (
2
) are to examine the points that are at a distance at most of the order of
n^{-\alpha}
around
x_{0}
. Given that the test function
f
is compactly supported, the points
\{x_{j}\}_{j}
, whose distance between
x_{0}
are of order far larger than
n^{-\alpha}
, will eventually fall outside the support of
f
as
n\to\infty
and only points within a distance of
x_{0}
of order at most
n^{-\alpha}
fall within the support. Note that if
\alpha=0
, the scaled linear statistics are reduced to the global linear statistics and ”sees” all points simultaneously. If
\alpha
is big enough such that
n^{\alpha}
is the microscopic scale around the point
x_{0}
, then we are at the local (or microscopic) regime, and the scaled linear statistics will only ”see” finitely many points in the limit. Hence, the scale
n^{\alpha}
, between the macroscopic and microscopic scales, is called the mesoscopic scale. Typically, in the bulk
\alpha\in(0,1)
, at the hard edge
\alpha\in(0,2)
, and at the soft edge
\alpha\in(0,2/3)
.

In this mesoscopic regime in the literature, the limiting fluctuations are studied by
[
46
]
for the classical compact groups. Later, the mesoscopic fluctuations in the bulk and at the soft edges for deformed Wigner matrix are studied by
[
35
]
, which includes GUE. The mesoscopic fluctuations for Dyson’s Brownian motion in the bulk are also studied in
[
17
]
via loop equations. The bulk universal mesoscopic fluctuations for OPEs that can be approximated by modified Jacobi ensembles are considered in
[
7
]
. The bulk mesoscopic fluctuations for the sparsely perturbed Jacobi Unitary Ensembles are considered in
[
39
]
. For Wigner matrices and
\beta
-ensembles, the bulk mesoscopic limit is shown in
[
32
]
. The bulk mesoscopic fluctuations for OPEs that priorly have a sine universality are considered in
[
31
]
. The bulk mesoscopic fluctuations of the linear statistics with smooth test functions (
f\in C_{c}^{6}
)for
\beta
ensembles with smooth potentials (
V\in C^{7}
as defined as (
7
)) are considered in
[
3
]
, by the loop equation technique. The mesoscopic fluctuations for circular orthogonal polynomial ensembles are considered in
[
9
]
. Recently, mesoscopic eigenvalue statistics of Wigner-type matrices in the bulk, at edges and at cusp-like singularities have been studied by
[
41
,
42
]
. By far, a substantial portion of research is concentrated about the bulk. Most methods designed for the bulk break down at edges, which makes the analysis more difficult. Hence, non-trivial modifications to these methods are needed to study the edge behaviour. This paper aims to address the gap in understanding the mesoscopic limit at the edges (both soft and hard). It is dedicated to establishing general conditions for such behaviour at the mesoscopic scale for orthogonal polynomial ensembles at the edges.

Here, we present our first two pedagogical results, which are consequences of our general theorems.

Non-varying Weights

A canonical example of a compactly supported non-varying measure is the modified Jacobi Unitary Ensemble

d\mu(x)=(1-x)^{\gamma_{1}}(1+x)^{\gamma_{2}}h(x)dx,\quad x\in[-1,1],

(3)

where
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}>-1
are parameters and
h
is an analytic function in a neighbourhood of
[-1,1]
and strictly positive on
[-1,1]
. The limiting distribution for the point process is the arcsine measure
d\rho_{\mu}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}dx
on
[-1,1]
. In the bulk, its mesoscopic limit has been studied by
[
7
]
, whose result directly implies that, for
f\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
,
x_{0}\in(-1,1)
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, as
n\to\infty
, the following converges in distribution

X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right]\to\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}\right)^{2}dxdy\right).

(4)

Our result is at the (hard) edges
x_{0}=1
or
-1
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mu
be the modified Jacobi weight as (
3
), given
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}>-1
. Then we have that, for all
f\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and
\alpha\in(0,2)
, the following converges in distribution as
n\to\infty

\displaystyle X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right]\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma^{2}_{f}\right)

(5)

\displaystyle\sigma_{f}^{2}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{f(-x^{2})-f(-y^{2})}{x-y}\right)^{2}dxdy,\qquad x_{0}=1\\
\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{f(x^{2})-f(y^{2})}{x-y}\right)^{2}dxdy,\qquad x_{0}=-1.\end{cases}

(6)

Theorem
1.1
is a consequence of more general results Theorem
2.1
or Theorem
2.3
, which we will discuss in Subsection
2.3
. The proof of Theorem
1.1
is presented in Section
8
.

Varying Weights

Let us consider the case where there exists a real potential
V
on
\mathbb{R}
such that

d\mu_{n}(x)=e^{-nV(x)}dx,\quad x\in\mathbb{R},

(7)

\frac{V(x)}{\log(x^{2}+1)}\to+\infty\quad\text{as }|x|\to\infty.

(8)

Condition (
8
) is to ensure all finite moments of
\mu_{n}
. This is a classical case where we are expecting soft edges, since the measure
\mu_{n}
is supported on the real line. The equilibrium measure with respect to the potential
V
is defined as the unique probability measure
\rho_{\mu}
that minimises the potential

I_{V}(\nu)\coloneqq-\int\int\log|x-y|d\nu(x)d\nu(y)+\int V(x)d\nu(x).

(9)

The existence and uniqueness of the minimum
I_{V}(\rho_{\mu})
is achieved via potential theory. For
V
being analytic and strictly convex, it is also known that
\rho_{\mu}
is supported in a single interval. For more about the potential theory, one may refer to
[
43
]
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume
d\mu_{n}(x)=e^{-nV(x)}dx
to be supported on the real line. Let
V
be an analytic and strictly convex potential satisfying (
8
). Without loss of generality, up to some normalisation, assume the equilibrium measure, which is the minimiser of (
9
), to be supported in a single interval
[-1,1]
. Then the same asymptotic result as (
5
) and (
6
) in Theorem
1.1
follows for all
f\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and
\alpha\in(0,\frac{2}{3})
, as
n\to\infty
.

Theorem
1.2
is a consequence of a general result Theorem
2.2
, which we will discuss in Subsection
2.3
. Analyticity and convexity are not the necessary conditions for the general results in this paper. However, they ensure the equilibrium measure to be supported on a single interval, see
[
13
]
. The proof of Theorem
1.2
is presented in Section
8
.

The conclusion of the results above holds significance in several aspects. First of all, the limiting variance (
6
) is scaling invariant. Set
g(x)=f(a^{2}x)
and we have
\sigma_{f}^{2}=\sigma_{g}^{2}
. This gives a heuristic explanation of the independence of
\alpha
in the limit. However, compared with the bulk mesoscopic limit in (
4
),
\sigma_{f}^{2}
is not translation invariant in
f
.

Finally, the variances for both the soft and the hard edges share the same formula (
6
) as indicated in the two theorems above, though microscopically, the OPEs may have different limiting processes as discussed. Our mesoscopic theorems do not require any knowledge about microscopic information a priori. It is, therefore, reasonable to anticipate that there is a broad range of OPEs for which the linear statistics should yield limiting mesoscopic fluctuations at the edges.

Note that Basor, Widom and Ehrhardt studied, in separate works, the asymptotics of the determinants of Airy and Bessel operators
[
1
,
2
]
. Their results imply that the linear statistics of the scaled Airy or Bessel point processes have the limiting Gaussian distribution with the variance same as (
6
), when zooming out. However, when applying their results to OPEs, for example GUE, one gets double limit asymptotics rather than the direct mesoscopic limits considered in this paper.

Our purpose of this paper is to extend Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
to a wider range of
\mu
and
\mu_{n}
. Analyticity and convexity of the potential
V
are not necessary. We include cases where
\mu
or
\mu_{n}
is a discrete measure, for example, Hahn polynomials (from the uniform measure on lozenge tilings of a hexagon), Krawtchouk polynomials (from the uniform measure on domino tilings on the Aztec diamond) and Tricomi-Carlitz polynomials in Subsection
10.5
. Our starting point is the three-term recurrence relation for the orthogonal polynomials. We give conditions on the coefficients that imply a mesoscopic CLT. Our criteria will be satisfied by the classical (properly scaled) hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials where the recurrence coefficients are explicitly known, cf.
[
25
]
. Our method is inspired by
[
7
,
8
]
, which use the recurrence coefficients to characterise the moments of the linear statistics of an OPE. The difficulty of adapting the methods of
[
7
]
is that the standard Combes-Thomas estimate to prove mesoscopic limit in bulk is unsuitable for the analysis at the edges (cf. Sections
3.4
). In this paper, we develop estimates at the edges in place of the Combes-Thomas estimate (cf. Propositions
4.1
and
4.3
). Moreover, our estimates work for the varying, as well as non-varying, weights, while Breuer and Duits only focus on the non-varying measure in
[
7
]
. A further ingredient is a quantitative determinate estimate for finite matrices that is approximately a Toeplitz (cf. Proposition
7.1
). This is related to Ehrhardt’s generalised Helton–Howe–Pincus formula (see (
307
)) which is used by
[
7
]
for semi-infinite Toeplitz operators. Last but not least, we directly analyse the mesoscopic linear statistics without assuming microscopic kernel universality.

2
Statement of Results

In this section, we will state our main results. The proofs are given in the later sections. The general results are described in terms of the three-term recurrence relations of orthogonal polynomials, which we will introduce first.

2.1
Orthonormal Polynomials and Recurrence Relations

Given a measure
\mu_{n}
on a subset of
\mathbb{R}
, with finite moments, we let
\{p_{j,n}(x)\}_{j\geq 0}
be the family of orthonormal polynomials with respect to
\mu_{n}
. The second subscript
n
emphasises that the measure
\mu_{n}
may be varying in
n
as pointed out by the examples from (
7
). Precisely, such
p_{j,n}
is the unique polynomial of degree
j
with a positive leading coefficient. For
\mu_{n}
is supported on continuous sets, we have

\int p_{j,n}(x)p_{k,n}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)=\delta_{j,k}.

(10)

For
\mu_{n}
supported on discrete sets, the integral above is understood as the corresponding sum over the mass points, namely,
\sum_{l=0}^{M_{n}}p_{j,n}(x_{l})p_{k,n}(x_{l})\mu_{n}(x_{l})=\delta_{j,k}
, where
M_{n}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. In the case
M_{n}<\infty
, we require
(M_{n}-n)n^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
to grow at least at the order of
n^{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
(cf. Condition
2.1
, which guarantees this requirement.).

It is well-known that orthonormal polynomials satisfy a three-term recurrence relation

\displaystyle xp_{0,n}(x)=

\displaystyle a_{1,n}p_{1,n}(x)+b_{0,n}p_{0,n}(x),

(11)

\displaystyle xp_{j,n}(x)=

\displaystyle a_{j+1,n}p_{j+1,n}(x)+b_{j,n}p_{j,n}(x)+a_{j,n}p_{j-1,n}(x),

(12)

for
j=1,2,\dots
and some coefficients
a_{j,n}>0
and
b_{j,n}\in\mathbb{R}
. These coefficients uniquely determine the orthonormal polynomials and are fundamental to our analysis. However, the recurrence coefficients do not, in general, determine the measure
\mu_{n}
uniquely. They do so only if
\mu_{n}
is determined by its moments. Throughout this paper, we do not assume that the corresponding moment problem is determinate.

For the non-varying weight
\mu
, the above discussion follows in the same way.

The asymptotic results of linear statistics of OPEs can be obtained by studying the recurrence coefficients. Among them, Breuer and Duits study the global fluctuations
[
8
]
. One case of
[
8
]
is that, given the existence

\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n+k,n}\eqqcolon a,\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}b_{n+k,n}\eqqcolon b,

(13)

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following converges in distribution, as
n\to\infty
,

X_{f}^{(n)}-\mathbb{E}[X_{f}^{(n)}]\to\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k\left|\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(2a\cos(\theta)+b)e^{ik\theta}d\theta\right|^{2}\right).

(14)

For the non-varying weights the assumption (
13
) can be simplified to
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}\eqqcolon a
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}b_{n}\eqqcolon b
. Compared with the mesoscopic limit in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, which are universal results, the variance of the limiting global fluctuations depends on the limits of the recurrence coefficients.

Consequently, the edges of the global fluctuations in this case are
b-2a
and
b+2a
.
One should be aware that though the edges of fluctuations coincide with the boundary of the equilibrium measure as in Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
, this is not the case in general. For example, the Tricomi Carlitz Polynomials, given in Subsection
10.5
, have the equilibrium measure supported on
\mathbb{R}
, while the fluctuations of the associated OPE take place on
[-2,2]
only.

Another important result about the limiting fluctuations of mesoscopic linear statistics in the bulk is obtained by
[
7
]
i.e., (
4
) holds, whenever the recurrence coefficients are such that

a_{j}=a+O(j^{-1}),\quad b_{j}=b+O(j^{-1}),\quad\text{ as }j\to\infty,

(15)

for some
a>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
. The recurrence relations also play a central role in the study of mesoscopic fluctuations of Circular OPEs and sparse perturbations of JUE in
[
9
,
39
]
respectively.

2.2
Results on Non-Varying Weights

Consider an OPE (
1
) with respect to the real Borel measure
\mu
with finite moments, where the associated recurrence coefficients are
a_{j}
and
b_{j}
, given by (
11
) and (
12
). Note that the recurrence coefficients do not have a second subscript, since they come from the measure
\mu
, non-varying in
n
.

Recall that
X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}
is the mesoscopic linear statistics of an OPE for a test function
f
around the point
x_{0}
which is defined by (
2
). Now we are going to state our general theorems for non-varying weights.

Theorem 2.1
.

Consider
0<\alpha<2
. Suppose there exist
a>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that the recurrence coefficients associated with the OPE satisfy

a_{j}=a+O(j^{-\alpha-\varepsilon}),\quad b_{j}=b+O(j^{-\alpha-\varepsilon})

(16)

for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1-\alpha/2)
small, as
j\to\infty
. Then, for any
f\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
, as
n\to\infty
, the following converges in distribution

\displaystyle X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right]\to\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}_{f}),

(17)

where

\displaystyle\sigma_{f}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{f(-x^{2})-f(-y^{2})}{x-y}\right)^{2}dxdy,\quad\text{ for }x_{0}=b+2a+o(n^{-\alpha}),

(18)

\displaystyle\sigma_{f}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{f(x^{2})-f(y^{2})}{x-y}\right)^{2}dxdy,\quad\text{ for }x_{0}=b-2a+o(n^{-\alpha}).

(19)

The assumption (
16
) is natural. Note that the Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind have constant recurrence coefficients, cf. Subsection
10.3
, which comes from the Jacobi Unitary Ensemble from random matrix theories. More generally, the modified Jacobi polynomials also satisfy (
16
) (cf. (
325
) due to
[
28
]
). Hence, Theorem
1.1
is a direct consequence of Theorem
2.1
. The proof is elaborated in Section
8
. Another interesting example is the OPE with a logarithm weight studied by
[
14
]
. The assumption (
16
) is also satisfied in this case, see Subsection
10.6
. Moreover, the assumption (
16
) with exactly the same rate of convergence is considered by
[
7
]
to show the bulk mesoscopic universality, i.e., (
4
), in their work.

Also note that the exact edges are at
b+2a
and
b-2a
. Theorem
2.1
allows
x_{0}
to be close to these edges as long as they have a distance of order at most
o(n^{-\alpha})
. This is also natural, since the mesoscopic linear statistics (
2
) is scaled as
n^{\alpha}
and any perturbations smaller than the window size should not change the result.

Another remark is that, Theorem
2.1
also holds for an OPE associated with a varying weight
\mu_{n}
with varying recurrence coefficients
a_{j,n}
and
b_{j,n}
such that

\sup\limits_{j\geq n-n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+\varepsilon}}|a_{j,n}-a|=O(n^{-\alpha-\varepsilon}),\quad\sup\limits_{j\geq n-n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+\varepsilon}}|b_{j,n}-b|=O(n^{-\alpha-\varepsilon}).

(20)

Though assumption (
20
) is slightly weaker than the assumption (
16
), in most reasonable examples of orthogonal polynomial ensembles, the measures of such are of bounded supports. Typically, there is no scaling with
n
in such cases. Hence, Theorem
2.1
is stated for non-varying cases. To maintain the consistency of the proofs in this paper, we will use (
20
) in the proof of Theorem
2.1
in Section
9
.

2.3
Results on Varying Weights

Now, we state the results about varying weights. Given an OPE (
1
) with respect to the real Borel measure
\mu_{n}
with finite moments, let the recurrence coefficients associated with measure
\mu_{n}
be
a_{j,n}
and
b_{j,n}
defined by (
11
) and (
12
).

Let
0<\alpha<2
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1-\frac{\alpha}{2})
small. Define
I_{n}^{(\alpha,\varepsilon)}
to be an indexing set

I_{n}^{(\alpha,\varepsilon)}\coloneqq\{j\in\mathbb{N}:n-n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+\varepsilon}\leq j\leq n+n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+\varepsilon}\},

(21)

In Theorems
2.2
and
2.3
, we will assume the recurrence coefficients are slowly varying as follows.

Condition 2.1
.

There exist absolute constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that, for all
j\in I_{n}^{(\alpha,\varepsilon)}
as (
21
) and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle c_{0}<|a_{j,n}|<c_{1}

\displaystyle,\quad|b_{j,n}|<c_{1},

(22)

\displaystyle|a_{j,n}-a_{j-1,n}|\leq\frac{c_{1}}{n}

\displaystyle,\quad|b_{j,n}-b_{j-1,n}|\leq\frac{c_{1}}{n}.

(23)

The parameter
\alpha
is the same as the ”scaling” parameter in the mesoscopic linear statistics. Condition
2.1
means that we only assume the recurrence coefficients of order around
n
with a window size of
2n^{\alpha/2+\varepsilon}
are bounded and slowly varying. As we will show, to prove the mesoscopic limit of the linear statistics, it is enough to consider recurrence coefficients of orders only inside this window. Many interesting examples in random matrix theories satisfy this condition and it is not hard to verify this. The recurrence coefficients from Theorem
1.2
will satisfy this condition, and so are those from the classical (hypergeometric) orthogonal polynomials along the Askey scheme when scaled (cf.
[
25
]
).

We will zoom in around a point
x_{0}
, that may depend on
n
, such that

x_{0}=b_{n-1,n}-2\sqrt{a_{n,n}a_{n-1,n}}+o(n^{-\alpha})\quad\text{on the left, or }x_{0}=b_{n-1,n}+2\sqrt{a_{n,n}a_{n-1,n}}+o(n^{-\alpha}),\quad\text{on the right}.

(24)

That is, for varying weights, instead of
b\pm 2a
in Theorem
2.1
, we centre
x_{0}
around the points
b_{n-1,n}\pm 2\sqrt{a_{n,n}a_{n-1,n}}
. We note that if
b_{n-1,n}-2\sqrt{a_{n,n}a_{n-1,n}}-x_{0}>c>0
, one can show with the same proof in this paper, the variance of
X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}
is
o(1)
. The same is true if
x_{0}
is right to the right edge. Also note that whenever (
13
) holds, this is the case where
x_{0}
is outside the global fluctuation range according to
[
8
]
. One can also see an example in Subsection
10.5
for further discussion.

Now we are going to state our general theorems for varying weights. Recall that
X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}
, defined in (
2
), is the mesoscopic linear statistics of an OPE for a test function
f
around the point
x_{0}
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Consider
0<\alpha<\frac{2}{3}
. Assume there is an
\varepsilon\in(0,1-\frac{\alpha}{2})
such that Condition
2.1
is satisfied for all
j\in I_{n}^{(\alpha,\varepsilon)}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, for any
f\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
, the following converges in distribution as
n\to\infty

\displaystyle X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right]\to\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}_{f}),

(25)

where

\displaystyle\sigma_{f}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{f(-x^{2})-f(-y^{2})}{x-y}\right)^{2}dxdy,\quad\text{ for }x_{0}=b_{n-1,n}+2\sqrt{a_{n,n}a_{n-1,n}}+o(n^{-\alpha}),

(26)

\displaystyle\sigma_{f}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{f(x^{2})-f(y^{2})}{x-y}\right)^{2}dxdy,\quad\text{ for }x_{0}=b_{n-1,n}-2\sqrt{a_{n,n}a_{n-1,n}}+o(n^{-\alpha}).

(27)

Theorem
2.2
only deals with the case
0<\alpha<\frac{2}{3}
. For soft edges with square root decay, this is optimal. But for hard edges, the result should also hold for
0<\alpha<2
. We will show that this is true under additional assumptions. Indeed, Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
and
2.2
are special cases of the following.

Theorem 2.3
.

Consider
0<\alpha<2
. Assume there is an
\varepsilon\in(0,1-\frac{\alpha}{2})
such that the Condition
2.1
is satisfied for all
j\in I_{n}^{(\alpha,\varepsilon)}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Also assume the following holds, as
n\to\infty

\max_{j\in I_{n}^{(\alpha,\varepsilon)}}\left|a_{j,n}a_{j-2,n}-a_{j-1,n}^{2}\right|=o(n^{-\alpha-\varepsilon}),

(28)

\max_{j\in I_{n}^{(\alpha,\varepsilon)}}\left|(|b_{j-1,n}-x_{0}|-a_{j,n})a_{j-2,n}-(|b_{j-2,n}-x_{0}|-a_{j-1,n})a_{j-1,n}\right|=o(n^{-\frac{3\alpha}{2}-\varepsilon}).

(29)

Then, for any
f\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
, as
n\to\infty
, the following converges in distribution

X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right]\to\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}_{f}),

(30)

where
\sigma_{f}^{2}
is given by (
26
) and (
27
).

Note that the Condition
2.1
implies that both left-hand sides of (
28
) and (
29
) are
O(n^{-1})
. In other words, Theorem
2.2
is a direct consequence of Theorem
2.3
considering
\alpha\in(0,\frac{2}{3})
.

These conditions (
28
) and (
29
) are technical in the proof. To understand where they come from, more background is needed. We will postpone the explanations to the end of Subsection
3.5
.

There are two examples that motivate conditions (
28
) and (
29
). First, it is shown in
[
28
]
that the recurrence coefficients of the modified Jacobi polynomials with measure (
3
) are slowly varying in a stronger sense, i.e.,

\displaystyle|a_{j}-a_{j-1}|=O(j^{-3}),\quad|b_{j}-b_{j-1}|=O(j^{-3}),\qquad\text{as }j\to\infty.

Note that there is no scaling in this model, and we remove the second subscript
n
from the notations. Hence, the conditions (
28
) and (
29
) are satisfied for all
0<\alpha<2
. The limit of mesoscopic fluctuations holds for all
\alpha\in(0,2)
, .i.e., Theorem
1.1
. For details, see proof of Theorem
1.1
in Section
8
.

Another motivation for the conditions is the Laguerre Unitary Ensemble, which exhibits a hard edge on the left (at
0
). In this case,
a_{j,n}=\sqrt{j(j+\gamma)}/n
,
b_{j,n}=(2j+\gamma+1)/n
and
x_{0}=0
. When
\gamma=0
, it is easy to verify that assumption (
28
) holds for all
0<\alpha<2
and the left-hand side of (
29
) vanishes for all
j
. Hence, Theorem
2.3
applies and we obtain the limit of mesoscopic fluctuations for all
\alpha\in(0,2)
at the hard (left) edge. For general
\gamma>0
, Theorem
2.3
also applies, see Subsection
10.1
for details. The Laguerre case demonstrates that a slowly varying condition in a stronger sense is not necessary. It suffices that the non-linear second-order combinations appearing in (
28
) and (
29
) vary sufficiently slowly.

Last but not the least, the assumption
f\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
in
Theorems
2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
is unlikely to be optimal. Indeed, the limiting variance
\sigma_{f}^{2}
suggests that
f\in H^{1/2+\delta}(\mathbb{R})
for some
\delta>0
and compactly supported. This extension is obtained for the bulk Wigner case
[
33
]
. To extend our results to
H^{1/2+\delta}(\mathbb{R})
, a uniform variance
estimate in
H^{1/2+\delta}
is needed to replace Proposition 5.1 of

[
7
]
(cf.
Proposition
4.6
). We do not pursue it here.

2.4
Overview of the Rest of the Paper

The rest of the paper is organised as follows.

In the Preliminaries, Section
3
, we introduce the cumulant expansion of OPEs. We will express it in terms of recurrence coefficients. Special care is needed in the case where the moment problem is indeterminate. We will also present the main tool of analysis, an estimate of a three-diagonal matrix with slowly varying entries and explain why the usual Combes-Thomas estimate is insufficient for our setup.

The varying weights are more difficult to prove than the non-varying ones. Hence, most of the content of this paper is to develop techniques for the varying weights, the proof of which we will show first.

In Section
4
, we provide the proof of Theorem
2.3
. We illustrate the essence of proof by demonstrating several key propositions. The proofs of these key propositions are deferred in later Sections
5
,
6
and
7
.

In Section
8
, we explore the conditions of Theorem
2.3
and subsequently prove Theorems
1.1
,
2.2
and
1.2
, in that specific order.

Theorem
2.1
requires a different strategy of proof than the other theorems, since it is essentially a result of non-varying weights. It is explained and proved in Section
9
.

In Section
10
, we give several examples that Theorems
2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
are applicable. There includes examples of continuous and discrete weights.

3
Preliminaries

In this section, we will introduce some notations and recall some preliminary facts regarding cumulants for linear statistics. We will follow the approach proposed by Breuer and Duits,
[
7
,
8
]
. They are the first to discover that the cumulants of the linear statistics can be expressed in terms of the Jacobi (semi-infinite) matrix corresponding to the measure
\mu_{n}
that defines the OPE. This discovery successfully leads to several results about linear statistics of OPEs, regarding both global and mesoscopic scales. This approach can also be applied to extended-OPEs that have extra parameters indicating the time transition, that rises naturally in many random tiling models, see
[
18
,
16
]
.

We will also introduce the Combes-Thomas estimate, which is an essential element proving the mesoscopic limit in the bulk,
[
7
]
. However, as we will explain in this section, at the edges, it is too rough and its improvement is required. To this end, we will introduce a formula for symmetric tri-diagonal matrices, that is well-known, but a key element for what follows.

3.1
Some Notations

We start this section with some notations that we will use. For a general reference see
[
45
]
. For a compact operator
A
on a (separable) Hilbert space, we denote the singular values by
\sigma_{j}(A)
, which are the square roots of the eigenvalues of the compact self-adjoint operator
A^{*}A
. Then we define

1.

\|A\|_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{j}\sigma_{j}(A)
to be the operator norm,

2.

\|A\|_{1}\coloneqq\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}(A)
to be the trace norm,

3.

\|A\|_{2}\coloneqq\sqrt{\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}(A)^{2}}
to be the Hilbert-Schmidt norm.

Then we have the following inequalities

1.

For
j=1,2,\infty
,

\|AB\|_{j}\leq\|A\|_{j}\|B\|_{\infty},\quad\|AB\|_{j}\leq\|A\|_{\infty}\|B\|_{j},

\|AB\|_{1}\leq\|A\|_{2}\|B\|_{2}.

2.

If
A
is trace class,

|\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}A|\leq\|A\|_{1}.

We will frequently view a semi-infinite matrix
A=\left((A)_{i,j}\right)_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
with entries
(A)_{i,j}\in\mathbb{C}
, as an operator on
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
. Then

\|A\|_{\infty}\leq\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\sup_{k}\left|(A)_{k,k+j}\right|,

\|A\|_{1}\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\left|(A)_{i,j}\right|,

\|A\|_{2}=\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}|(A)_{i,j}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

If
A
is further Hermitian,
\eta\in\mathbb{C}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\eta\neq 0
and
\mathrm{Id}
is the identity operator, the following holds

\|(A-\eta\mathrm{Id})^{-1}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\eta|}.

(31)

Note that among many trace norm inequalities, the one above may not be the optimal nor elegant one. However, in our cases, it is sufficient.

3.2
Bounded and Unbounded Jacobi Operators

Let the Jacobi matrix
\mathcal{J}
be an semi-infinite tri-diagonal matrix associated with the measure
\mu_{n}
with entries being the recurrence coefficients defined as (
11
) and (
12
), i.e.,

\mathcal{J}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}b_{0,n}&a_{1,n}\\
a_{1,n}&b_{1,n}&a_{2,n}\\
&a_{2,n}&b_{2,n}&a_{3,n}&\\
&&a_{3,n}&b_{3,n}&a_{4,n}&\\
&&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{pmatrix}.

(32)

This
\mathcal{J}
is also called the Jacobi operator associated with the measure
\mu_{n}
, viewed as a linear operator on
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
with initial domain to be the space of finitely supported sequences. For finitely supported
\mu_{n}
(like for Krawchuk polynomials), then
J
is, in fact, a finite matrix, which can be embedded into a semi-infinite matrix adjoining zero rows and columns. It is a bounded linear operator only if both sequences
\{a_{j,n}\}_{j}
and
\{b_{j,n}\}_{j}
are bounded. For example,
\mathcal{J}
is bounded for the Chebyshev polynomials where the recurrence coefficients are constants. However, in many cases,
\mathcal{J}
is not necessarily a bounded operator. For example,
\mathcal{J}
is unbounded for the Hermite and Laguerre polynomials where
a_{j,n}
tends to infinity as
j\to\infty
. These examples are included in Section
10
. Breuer and Duits studied the resolvent of bounded
\mathcal{J}
and they successfully obtained the mesoscopic fluctuations in the bulk for the non-varying cases
[
7
]
. However, though
\mathcal{J}
is symmetric with all entries real numbers, its resolvent is only well-defined if
\mathcal{J}
is a self-adjoint operator. However,
\mathcal{J}
is essentially self-adjoint, if and only if the measure
\mu_{n}
is fully characterised by its moments, see Theorems
6.10.
and
6.16
in
[
44
]
. In terms of recurrence coefficients, that is if
\sum_{j\geq 1}a_{j,n}^{-1}=+\infty
, the moment problem is determinate. If
\{b_{j,n}\}_{j}
is bounded,
a_{j-1,n}a_{j+1,n}\leq a_{j,n}^{2}
for all
j\geq j_{0}
for some
j_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\sum_{j\geq 1}a_{j,n}^{-1}<+\infty
, then the moment problem is indeterminate. See Corollary
6.19.
in
[
44
]
and Theorem
1.5
in
[
4
]
. For example, for Freud weight
d\mu_{n}(x)=e^{-n|x|^{\gamma}}dx
,
\gamma>0
, shown in
[
27
,
36
]
, the recurrence coefficients have the asymptotics as

a_{j,n}=c_{\gamma}\left(\frac{j}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(1+O(j^{-1})),\quad\text{as }j\to\infty,\quad\text{and }b_{j,n}=0,

(33)

where
c_{\gamma}
is an universal constant only depending on
\gamma
. For a precise statement, see Section
10.4
. Hence, its Jacobi operator is essentially self-adjoint if and only if
\gamma\geq 1
.

In the rest of the paper, we will avoid calling a general
\mathcal{J}
an operator and only treat it as a semi-infinite tri-diagonal matrix, which is always well-defined as (
32
). We will construct a bounded linear operator from
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
to
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
associated with
\mathcal{J}
, that will be suitable for our further analysis.

Let
P(x;n)
be the column vector of the orthonormal polynomials

P(x;n)=(p_{0,n}(x),p_{1,n}(x),\cdots)^{T},

(34)

where the superscript
T
is the transpose of a vector. We define a semi-infinite matrix for any
l=1,2,\dots

G_{z}\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x-z)^{-1}P(x;n)P(x;n)^{T}d\mu_{n}(x),\quad z\in\mathbb{C},\quad\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z\neq 0.

(35)

Note that each entry of
G_{z}
is well-defined and bounded, i.e.,

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x-z)^{-1}p_{j,n}(x)p_{k,n}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right|\leq\frac{1}{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|p_{j,n}(x)p_{k,n}(x)\right|d\mu_{n}(x)\\
\leq\frac{1}{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|p_{j,n}(x)\right|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|p_{k,n}(x)\right|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1}{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|},

(36)

where we use the fact
\left|(x-z)^{-1}\right|\leq\frac{1}{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|}
for the first inequality, the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality for the second inequality and the normality of the orthonormal polynomials for the last equality. More generally, we have the following.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any
\mathbb{C}
-valued function
f
such that
\|f\|_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|<\infty
, the following represents a bounded linear operator from
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
to
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
, given the canonical basis in
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})

\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)P(x;n)P(x;n)^{T}d\mu_{n}(x).

(37)

Its operator norm is bounded by
\|f\|_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Define the multiplication operator
\mathcal{M}_{f}:L^{2}(\mu_{n})\to L^{2}(\mu_{n})
via
\mathcal{M}_{f}h(x)=f(x)h(x)
, for
h\in Dom(\mathcal{M}_{f})\coloneqq\{h\in L^{2}(\mu_{n}):\mathcal{M}_{f}h\in L^{2}(\mu_{n})\}
. The multiplication operator
\mathcal{M}_{f}
is self-adjoint and hence its operator norm is bounded by
\left\|\mathcal{M}_{f}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}
.

Denote (
37
) as
K_{f}
. Let
e_{j}=(0,0,\dots,1,0,0,\dots)^{T}
be the
j
-th vector of the canonical basis for
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
, i.e., the sequence with only the
j
-th element to be
1
and the rests are zeros. Then, each entry of (
37
) equals to

(e_{j},K_{f}e_{k})_{l^{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)p_{j-1,n}(x)p_{k-1,n}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{M}_{f}p_{j-1,n}(x)p_{k-1,n}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)=\left(\mathcal{M}_{f}p_{j-1,n},p_{k-1,n}\right)_{L^{2}(\mu_{n})}.

(38)

Let the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{n}}\coloneqq\overline{\mathrm{span}\{p_{j,n}:j\geq 0\}}^{L^{2}(\mu_{n})}
be the closure of the linear space spanned by the orthonormal polynomials. Clearly,
L^{2}(\mu_{n})=\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{n}}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{n}}^{\perp}
. Let
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(\mu_{n})\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{n}}
be the orthogonal projection operator onto
\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{n}}
. Define an unitary operator
\mathcal{U}:\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{n}}\mapsto l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
such that
\mathcal{U}p_{j,n}=e_{j+1}
. Then (
38
) shows that

K_{f}=\mathcal{U}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{M}_{f}\mathcal{U}^{-1}.

(39)

Therefore, (
37
) is a bounded operator and its operator norm
\|K_{f}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mathcal{M}_{f}\ |_{\infty}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}
.
∎

Lemma
3.1
shows that
G_{z}
defined in (
35
) is a bounded linear operator. By the three-term recurrence relation (
11
) and (
12
),
G_{z}
is the algebraic inverse of
\mathcal{J}-z
, i.e., the following holds entry-wise,

\left((\mathcal{J}-z)G_{z}\right)_{j,k}=\left(G_{z}(\mathcal{J}-z)\right)_{j,k}=\begin{cases}1,\quad&j=k,\\
0,\quad&j\neq k.\end{cases}

(40)

For any bounded operator
A:l^{2}\mapsto l^{2}
whose inverse
A^{-1}
exists and is also a bounded linear operator, the following holds algebraically, i.e., the following holds entry-wise as a semi-infinite matrix,

G_{z}-A^{-1}=G_{z}(A-\mathcal{J}+z)A^{-1}.

(41)

Note that
\mathcal{J}
is a tri-diagonal matrix, and hence
\mathcal{J}A^{-1}
is always well-defined as a semi-infinite matrix. If further
\mathcal{J}-z-A
represents a bounded linear operator from
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
to
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
, the right-hand side of (
41
) can be viewed as the operator compositions. In such case, with an abuse of terminology, we call (
41
) the resolvent identity.

The bounded operator
G_{z}
agrees with the resolvent of
\mathcal{J}
, whenever
\mathcal{J}
is self-adjoint. In such case,
G_{z}=(\mathcal{J}-z)^{-1}
.

3.3
Cumulants via Recurrence Relations

For a real-valued random variable
X
, whose moments are all finite, the cumulants
\mathcal{C}_{m}(X)
are uniquely defined by the cumulant generating function (if exists), for
t\in\mathbb{C}
in a neighbourhood of
0
,

\log\mathbb{E}[e^{tX}]=\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{t^{m}}{m!}\mathcal{C}_{m}(X).

From this expression, the moments of
X
can be recovered from its cumulants and vice versa. For example, the mean and variance of
X
are given by
\mathcal{C}_{1}(X)
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}(X)
respectively. Such moment-cumulant relationship can also be used to define cumulants, if the cumulant generating function does not exist. Note also that, if
X
follows a Gaussian distribution, we have
\log\mathbb{E}[e^{tX}]=t\mathcal{C}_{1}(X)+t^{2}\mathcal{C}_{2}(X)/2
. Later we will show that the cumulant expansion is absolutely convergent in our setup (cf.Lemma
3.4
).

Hence, to show that the linear statistics
X_{f,\alpha,x_{o}}^{(n)}
of an OPE as defined as (
2
) converges to a Gaussian distribution for a suitable class of test functions
f
, it is sufficient for us to study the asymptotic behaviour of its cumulants. First, we consider the test function
f
to be

f(x)=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\sum_{r=1}^{M}d_{r}\frac{1}{x-\lambda_{r}}

(42)

where
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
d_{r}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}
takes the imaginary part of a complex number, and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda_{r}>0
. We point out that any compactly supported and continuously differentiable function can be well approximated by such
f
in a certain Lipschitz space. This claim will be explained in Lemma
4.1
, which is a result in
[
7
]
.

Denote
\overline{\lambda}_{r}
to be the complex conjugate of
\lambda_{j}
. Then
f
can be rewritten as

f(x)=\sum_{r=1}^{2M}c_{r}\frac{1}{x-\eta_{r}},\qquad\\
\text{ where}\quad c_{r}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{d_{r}}{2i}\qquad r=1,\dots,M\\
-\frac{d_{r-M}}{2i}\quad r=1+M,\dots,2M\\
\end{cases}\eta_{r}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\lambda_{r}\quad r=1,\dots,M\\
\overline{\lambda}_{r-M}\qquad r=1+M,\dots,2M\\
\end{cases}.

(43)

and, for some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, define

f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)\coloneqq f(n^{\alpha}(x-x_{0})).

(44)

f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)P(x;n)P(x;n)^{T}d\mu_{n}(x)=\sum_{r=1}^{2M}\frac{c_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}G_{x_{0}+\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}},

(45)

\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)\right)^{l}P(x;n)P(x;n)^{T}d\mu_{n}(x),\quad l=1,2,\dots

(46)

where
G_{x_{0}+\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}}
is defined as (
35
). Similar to the properties of
G_{z}
, in general, the following holds for (
46
).

Corollary 3.1
.

\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}
is a well defined semi-infinite matrix and its entries are real and symmetric, i.e.,

\left(\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}\right)_{j,k}=\left(\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}\right)_{k,j}=\overline{\left(\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}\right)_{k,j}},\quad j,k\in\mathbb{N},

(47)

where the overline is the complex conjugate. Moreover,
f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})
represents a bounded linear operator from
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
to
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
. Its operator norm is estimated by

\left\|\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left(\sum_{r=1}^{2M}\left|\frac{c_{r}}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\eta_{r}}\right|\right)^{l}.

(48)

Proof.

The statement follows directly from Lemma
3.1
.
∎

Breuer and Duits obtained a beautiful formula of the cumulant generating function of the linear statistics in terms of
\mathcal{J}
, see the preliminaries of
[
7
]
. They showed the formula for
\mathcal{J}
being a bounded semi-infinite matrix. By Corollary
3.1
, we now extend their result to general
\mathcal{J}
, which can be unbounded.

Lemma 3.2

(Breuer-Duits)
.

The cumulant generating function for the linear statistics
X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}
of an OPE has the following determinantal structure,

\log\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(tX_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right)\right]=\log\det\left(\int e^{tf_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)}p_{j-1,n}(x)p_{i-1,n}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right)_{j,k=1}^{n},

(49)

where
f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})
is defined as (
45
),
\mathrm{Id}
is the identity operator and
P_{n}
is the canonical projection onto the first
n
coordinates.

Proof.

Note that the Vandermonde determinant admits the following formula

\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{i}-x_{j})=\frac{1}{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j-1,n}}\det(p_{j-1,n}(x_{i}))_{i,j=1}^{n}

(50)

where
p_{j,n}
is the orthonormal polynomial and
\gamma_{j,n}
is its leading coefficient.

Hence, use (
1
) to obtain that

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(tX_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{Z_{n}}\int\cdots\int\prod_{i=n}^{n}e^{tf_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x_{i})}\left(\det(p_{j-1,n}(x_{i}))_{i,j=1}^{n}\right)^{2}d\mu_{n}(x_{1})\cdots d\mu_{n}(x_{n})\\
=\frac{1}{Z_{n}}\int\cdots\int\det(e^{tf_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x_{i})}p_{j-1,n}(x_{i}))_{i,j=1}^{n}\det(p_{j-1,n}(x_{i}))_{i,j=1}^{n}d\mu_{n}(x_{1})\cdots d\mu_{n}(x_{n}),

(51)

where
Z_{n}>0
is some normalising constant.
Recall the Andrésief’s identity, for a measure
\mu
and measurable functions
f_{j},g_{j}\in L^{2}(\mu)
for
j=1,\dots n
,

\int\cdots\int\det\left(f_{j}(x_{k})\right)_{j,k=1}^{n}\det\left(g_{j}(x_{k})\right)_{j,k=1}^{n}d\mu(x_{1})\cdots d\mu(x_{n})=n!\det\left(\int f_{j}(x)g_{k}(x)d\mu(x)\right)_{j,k=1}^{n}.

(52)

Then,

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(tX_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right)\right]=\frac{n!}{Z_{n}}\det\left(\int e^{tf_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)}p_{j-1,n}(x)p_{i-1,n}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right)_{j,k=1}^{n}.

(53)

In particular, take
t=0
to obtain
\mathbb{E}\left[1\right]=\frac{n!}{Z_{n}}
. Hence
Z_{n}=n!
. This concludes the Lemma.
∎

Breuer and Duits also computed the cumulants in Sections
2.2
and
2.3
of
[
7
]
, which we extend and formulate as the following lemma. We also give the proof for completeness.

Lemma 3.3

(Breuer-Duits)
.

The cumulants for the linear statistics
X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}
of an OPE can be expressed to be

\mathcal{C}_{1}(X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)})=\mathbb{E}[X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}]=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(P_{n}f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})P_{n}\right),

(54)

where
P_{n}
is the canonical projection onto the first
n
coordinates. For
m\geq 2

\mathcal{C}_{m}(X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)})=m!\sum_{j=2}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}\sum_{l_{1}+\dots+l_{j}=m,l_{i}\geq 1}\\
\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{1})}P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{2})}P_{n}\dots\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{j})}P_{n}\right)-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(m)}P_{n}\right)}{l_{1}!\cdots l_{j}!},

(55)

where
\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}
is defined in (
46
).

Proof.

Note that for any non-singular square matrix
A
, we have
\log\det A=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\log A
. Then for
t
sufficiently small, by Lemma
3.2
, we have

\log\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(tX_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right)\right]=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\log\left(1+\int(e^{tf_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)}-1)p_{j-1,n}(x)p_{i-1,n}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right)_{j,k=1}^{n}.

(56)

we expand the
\log
and
\exp
and reorder the summations to obtain,

\log\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(tX_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right)\right]=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}\sum_{l_{1}\geq 1,l_{2}\geq 1,\dots,l_{j}\geq 1}\frac{t^{l_{1}+l_{2}+\dots+l_{j}}}{l_{1}!l_{2}!\dots l_{j}!}P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{1})}P_{n}\dots\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{j})}P_{n}\\
=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=j}^{\infty}t^{m}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}\sum_{l_{1}+\dots+l_{j}=m,l_{j}\geq 1}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{1})}P_{n}\dots\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{j})}P_{n}\right)}{l_{1}!l_{2}!\dots l_{j}!}\\
=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}t^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}\sum_{l_{1}+\dots+l_{j}=m,l_{j}\geq 1}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{1})}P_{n}\dots\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{j})}P_{n}\right)}{l_{1}!l_{2}!\dots l_{j}!}.

(57)

Then by expanding the right-hand side of (
57
), the cumulants of the linear statistics can be written as,

\mathcal{C}_{m}(X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)})=m!\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}\sum_{l_{1}+\dots+l_{j}=m,l_{i}\geq 1}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{1})}P_{n}\dots\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l_{j})}P_{n}\right)}{l_{1}!\cdots l_{j}!}.

(58)

Similarly to (
57
), by expanding the logarithm and exponential and reordering the summations, we obtain

\log(1+(e^{x}-1))=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j}\sum_{l_{1}\geq 1,l_{2}\geq 1,\dots,l_{j}\geq 1}\frac{x^{l_{1}+\dots+l_{j}}}{l_{1}!\cdots l_{j}!}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=j}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j}\sum_{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{j}=m,l_{i}\geq 1}\frac{x^{m}}{l_{1}!\cdots l_{j}!}\\
=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}x^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j}\sum_{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{j}=m,l_{i}\geq 1}\frac{1}{l_{1}!\cdots l_{j}!},

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that
\log(1+(e^{x}-1))=x
. Hence,
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j}\sum_{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{j}=m,l_{i}\geq 1}\frac{1}{l_{1}!\cdots l_{j}!}=0
for all
m\geq 2
.
Then, for
m\geq 2
, (
58
) can be rewritten to be (
55
)
∎

The extra term
\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(m)}P_{n}\right)
enables us to have good estimates of the cumulants. We will use this extensively to show that the cumulants of the linear statistics of an OPE only depend on the recurrence coefficients of order around
n
, given assumptions of Theorem
2.3
(cf. Proposition
4.4
). Another consequence is the following lemma, which is a by-product of Lemma
2.2
in
[
6
]
.

Lemma 3.4

(Breuer-Duits)
.

We have for
m\geq 2

\left|\mathcal{C}_{m}(X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)})\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}m!m^{3/2}\left\|f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right\|_{\infty}^{m-2}e^{m}\mathcal{C}_{2}(X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}),

(59)

where
\left\|f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\sum_{r=1}^{2M}\left|\frac{c_{r}}{\eta_{r}}\right|
. In particular, for
|t|<\left(e\left\|f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\right\|_{\infty}\right)^{-1}
, there exists some constant
c>0
such that

\sum_{m\geq 2}\left|\frac{t^{m}}{m!}\mathcal{C}_{m}(X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)})\right|\leq ct^{2}\mathcal{C}_{2}(X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}).

(60)

Proof.

Recall (
39
) in the proof of Lemma
3.1
, we have

\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}=\mathcal{U}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathcal{M}_{f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}}\right)^{l}\mathcal{U}^{-1}.

(61)

Define
K_{n}:L^{2}(\mu_{n})\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{n}}
such that
K_{n}\coloneqq\mathcal{U}^{-1}P_{n}\mathcal{U}\mathcal{P}
, which is a projection operator onto the space spanned by
\{p_{j,n}:j=0,\dots,n-1\}
. Then we rewrite the summand of (
55
) to be

\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(\left(\mathcal{M}_{f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}}\right)^{l_{1}}K_{n}\left(\mathcal{M}_{f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}}\right)^{l_{2}}K_{n}\dots\left(\mathcal{M}_{f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}}\right)^{l_{j}}K_{n}\right)-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(\left(\mathcal{M}_{f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}}\right)^{m}K_{n}\right)}{l_{1}!\cdots l_{j}!}.

(62)

In particular, we have
\mathcal{C}_{2}(X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)})=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{M}_{f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}},K_{n}\right]\left[K_{n},\mathcal{M}_{f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}}\right]
, where
[,]
is the commutator. From this, we use
(2.6)
in the proof of Lemma
2.2
in
[
6
]
to conclude (
59
) and the rest follows directly by the same proof as Lemma
2.2
in
[
6
]
.
∎

Rather than studying the series directly, Lemma
3.4
allows us to study the asymptotics of each cumulant as
n\to\infty
and to apply the dominated convergence theorem to the series.

In the case that the moment problem is determined we have the resolvent to be well-defined and
\left(\mathcal{J}-z\right)^{-1}=G_{z}
. Hence, the formula can be further simplified. We have by the three-term recurrence relation that
\left(G_{z}\right)^{(l)}=(\mathcal{J}-z)^{-l}
. Furthermore,
f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})=\sum_{r=1}^{2M}\frac{c_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\left(\mathcal{J}-x_{0}-\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\right)^{-1},
and

\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}=\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{l}.

(63)

For any linear operator
\mathcal{A}
, we define for
m\geq 2
,

\mathcal{C}_{m}^{(n)}(\mathcal{A})\coloneqq m!\sum_{j=2}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}\sum_{l_{1}+\dots+l_{j}=m,l_{i}\geq 1}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\mathcal{A})^{l_{1}}P_{n}\dots(\mathcal{A})^{l_{j}}P_{n}-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\mathcal{A}^{m}P_{n})}{l_{1}!\dots l_{j}!}.

(64)

Then it is clear that if we define
F\coloneqq n^{\alpha}f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})
, Then, by definition (
45
),
F=\sum_{r=1}^{2M}c_{r}G_{x_{0}+\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}}
, which is a bounded linear operator due to Corollary
3.1
. Then, for
m\geq 2
,

\mathcal{C}_{m}(X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)})=\mathcal{C}^{(n)}_{m}(n^{-\alpha}F)=n^{-m\alpha}\mathcal{C}^{(n)}_{m}(F),

where
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}_{m}
on the right-hand side is given by (
64
). The study of the cumulants of the mesoscopic linear statistics for large
n
, then, boils down to the asymptotic behaviour of
G_{x_{0}+\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}}
, defined in (
35
).

In the case that
\mathcal{J}
is not essentially self-adjoint, the relation (
63
) may not be true, though both hand sides are well defined. To obtain a similar formula, one needs to control the boundary term carefully. Generally, we have the following.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
N\geq n
, Define
\mathcal{J}_{N}\coloneqq P_{N}\mathcal{J}P_{N}+Q_{N}
. Then we have

P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}=P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J}_{N})\right)^{l}-\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\sum_{r=1}^{2M}P_{n}f^{j}(\mathcal{J}_{N})R_{r}B_{r,n}^{(l-1-j)},

(65)

where we let
E_{N}
be a semi-infinite matrix with only one entry non-zero
(E_{N})_{i,j}\coloneqq\mathrm{1}_{i=N,j=N+1}
and

R_{r}\coloneqq\frac{a_{N,n}}{n^{\alpha}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{N}-x_{0}-\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\right)^{-1}E_{N},

(66)

and
B_{r,n}^{(l-1-j)}
is a bounded linear operator explicitly by (
72
), whose operator norm is
\mathcal{O}(1)
uniformly in
n
.

Proof.

Rewrite the three-term recurrence relations (
11
) and (
12
) into a matrix form

xP_{N}P(x;n)=P_{N}\mathcal{J}_{N}P(x;n)+a_{N,n}E_{N}P(x;n),

(67)

where the term
a_{N,n}E_{N}P(x;n)
comes from the recurrence relation of the last entry, i.e.,
xp_{N-1,n}(x)
. Note that
\mathcal{J}_{N}
is a bounded self-adjoint operator and its resolvent is well defined. Hence we have for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z\neq 0
,

(x-z)^{-1}P_{N}P(x;n)=P_{N}(\mathcal{J}_{N}-z)^{-1}P(x;n)-(x-z)^{-1}a_{N,n}(\mathcal{J}_{N}-z)^{-1}E_{N}P(x;n).

(68)

Then by (
68
), the definition of
f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}
(
44
),
R_{r}
as (
66
), and linearity, we have

f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)P_{N}P(x;n)=P_{N}f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J}_{N})P(x;n)-\sum_{r=1}^{2M}R_{r}P(x;n)\frac{c_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\left(x-x_{0}-\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\right)^{-1}.

(69)

Then by either repeating (
69
)
l
times or using induction argument, we obtain

\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)\right)^{l}P_{N}P(x;n)\\
=P_{N}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J}_{N})\right)^{l}P(x;n)-\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\sum_{r=1}^{2M}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J}_{N})\right)^{j}R_{r}P(x;n)\frac{c_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\left(x-x_{0}-\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\right)^{-1}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)\right)^{l-1-j}.

(70)

Plug (
70
) into the definition (
46
). Then for any
N\geq n
, we have

P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(l)}=P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J}_{N})\right)^{l}-\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\sum_{r=1}^{2M}P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J}_{N})\right)^{j}R_{r}B_{r,n}^{(l-1-j)},

(71)

where

B_{r,n}^{(l-1-j)}\coloneqq\int\frac{c_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\left(x-x_{0}-\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\right)^{-1}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)\right)^{l-1-j}P(x;n)P(x;n)^{T}d\mu_{n}(x),

(72)

which, by Lemma
3.1
, is well-defined and bounded linear operator such that

\left\|B_{r,n}^{(l-1-j)}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\sup_{x}\left|\frac{c_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\left(x-x_{0}-\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}\right)^{-1}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(x)\right)^{l-1-j}\right|\leq\left|\frac{c_{r}}{\eta_{r}}\right|\left(\sum_{r^{\prime}}\left|\frac{c_{r}^{\prime}}{\eta_{r}^{\prime}}\right|\right)^{l-1-j}.

.
∎

The significance of Lemma

3.5
is that though the left-hand side of (
65
) still depends on the orthogonal measure
\mu_{n}
, it can be approximated by finitely many recurrence coefficients at the cost of the boundary terms. As we will show later that with a carefully chosen
N
, the term
P_{n}f^{j}(\mathcal{J}_{N})R_{r}
in Lemma
3.5
is to be exponentially small in the trace norm. Hence,
\mathcal{C}_{m}(X_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)})
can be approximated well enough by
\mathcal{C}_{m}^{(n)}(\mathcal{A})
as (
64
) with a carefully chosen
\mathcal{A}
(cf. Proposition
4.2
).

3.4
Combes–Thomas Estimate and its Limitation

In our analysis, we will need to estimate entries of the resolvent that are far away from the main diagonal. One way of doing that (which is successful in
[
7
]
) is by the Combes-Thomas estimate
[
10
]
. However, for our purpose, this Combes-Thomas estimate is not sufficient, and we will use conditions in Theorem
2.3
to improve the estimate. Before doing so, we will recall the Combes-Thomas estimate and show its limitations for our purposes.

Combes–Thomas Estimate

Given
\eta\in\mathbb{C}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\eta\neq 0
. Consider

J=\begin{pmatrix}b_{0}&a_{1}\\
a_{1}&b_{1}&a_{2}\\
&a_{2}&b_{2}&a_{3}\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{pmatrix}-\left(x_{0}+\frac{\eta}{n^{\alpha}}\right)\mathrm{Id},

(73)

where
b_{j},x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<a_{j}\leq c
for some constant
c>0
and
\mathrm{Id}
is the identity operator. Then for any
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\left|\left(J^{-1}\right)_{j,k}\right|\leq\frac{2n^{\alpha}}{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\eta|}e^{-\min\{1,\frac{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\eta|}{4ecn^{\alpha}}\}|j-k|}.

(74)

For a proof of the Combes–Thomas estimate, see
[
10
]
or Proposition 2.3. in
[
7
]
.

Note that the Combes-Thomas estimate works for both varying
a_{j,n}
and
b_{j,n}
and non-varying
a_{j}
and
b_{j}
. Here we omit the second subscript for the convenience of following discussions.

The Combes-Thomas estimate is a useful result and is successful in studying the bulk universality for OPEs, see
[
7
]
. However, in our setup, it has some major limitations. First of all,
a_{j}
or
a_{j,n}
can be unbounded. In case of the scaled Hermite polynomials (corresponding to the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble of the random matrix theory) we have
a_{j,n}=\sqrt{\frac{j}{n}}
and
b_{j,n}=0
. However, in the cumulant expansion (
55
), one only needs to estimate
J^{-l}P_{n}
. The following argument enables us to replace
J
by a truncated version. Take
l=1
for example. Take
N=n+\delta
for some
\delta>0
and define

J_{N}\coloneqq P_{N}JP_{N}+Q_{N}.

By the resolvent identity and the trace norm inequality, we have

\|(J_{N}^{-1}-J^{-1})P_{n}\|_{1}\leq\|J^{-1}\|_{\infty}\|(J-J_{N})J_{N}^{-1}P_{n}\|_{1}.

(75)

Recall that
J_{N}
is a block matrix and
N>n
. Hence,
J_{N}^{-1}P_{n}=P_{N}J_{N}^{-1}P_{n}
. Also, recall that
J
and
J_{N}
are tri-diagonal matrices. Hence, the only non-zero entry of
(J-J_{N})P_{N}
is the
N+1,N
-th entry whose value is
a_{N}
. Now we estimate the trace norm with entries, i.e.,

\|(J-J_{N})J_{N}^{-1}P_{n}\|_{1}=\|(J-J_{N})P_{N}J_{N}^{-1}P_{n}\|_{1}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|a_{N}\left(J_{N}^{-1}\right)_{N,k}\right|.

(76)

Then, we only need to estimate
\left(J_{N}^{-1}\right)_{j,k}
for
j=N
and
k=1,2,\dots,n
. We use the Combes-Thomas estimate for
J_{N}
to conclude that (
76
) is exponentially small if
\delta\gg n^{\alpha}
. Note that
\|J^{-1}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{n^{\alpha}}{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\eta|}
, and hence (
75
) is exponentially small as well. Recall that the cumulant formula given by (
64
). This implies that the difference of the second cumulants
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}_{2}(J^{-1})
and
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}_{2}(J_{N}^{-1})
is exponentially small. The cumulants of higher order can also be compared in a similar way. The truncation
J_{N}
can be a good estimate of
J
in the cumulant.

However, this reveals a second limitation. This argument works well for
\delta=n^{\beta}
and
0<\alpha<\beta<1
, which is not good enough near the edge. In some examples, for instance, the modified Jacobi case, we want to zoom in further and need an estimate that holds for any
0<\alpha<2
.

Note that if
J
is a Toeplitz operator, the Combes-Thomas estimate can be improved near the edges. For example, take
b_{j,n}=0
and
a_{j,n}=1
, which are the recurrence coefficients for Chebyshev polynomials. Take
x_{0}=2
. One can use Wiener-Hopf factorisation to compute the resolvent explicitly, that is

\left(J^{-1}\right)_{j,k}=\frac{\omega(\eta)}{1-\omega(\eta)^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega(\eta)^{|j-k|}}-\frac{1}{\omega(\eta)^{j+k}}\right),

(77)

where

\omega(\eta)\coloneqq\frac{2+\frac{\eta}{n^{\alpha}}+\left(\left(2+\frac{\eta}{n^{\alpha}}\right)^{2}-4\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}.

We take the principal square root so that
|\omega(\eta)|>1
.
Hence, we estimate,

\left|\left(J^{-1}\right)_{j,k}\right|\leq d_{1}n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}e^{-d_{2}n^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}|j-k|},

(78)

for some constants
d_{1},d_{2}>0
. Hence, we say that for
|j-k|>n^{\beta}
with
0<\frac{\alpha}{2}<\beta<1
,
\left|\left(J^{-1}\right)_{j,k}\right|
is exponentially small. One should note that
\frac{\alpha}{2}<\beta
is possible since the spectral density of
J
vanishes at the edge of the spectrum as a square root. If
x_{0}\in(-2,2)
is chosen, we will get that the entries are exponentially small for all
0<\alpha<\beta<1
.

Therefore, these limitations suggest a finer estimation about
J_{N}^{-1}
, which should resemble (
78
), for some special class of
J
. In a nutshell, the Combes-Thomas estimate works for general Jacobi matrices, but can be improved under further conditions.

3.5
Inverse of a Symmetric Tri-diagonal Matrix

Recall that
J_{N}
is a tri-diagonal symmetric matrix. To find the inverse of such a matrix is a classic algebraic question and can be reduced to computing the first and last columns of
J_{N}^{-1}
. For a review, see
[
38
]
. Here we only present the expressions needed.

Let us look at some general properties of an
N\times N
non singular symmetric tri-diagonal matrix with
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R},b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

J_{N}=\begin{pmatrix}b_{0}&a_{1}\\
a_{1}&b_{1}&a_{2}\\
&a_{2}&b_{2}&a_{3}\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\\
&&&a_{N-2}&b_{N-2}&a_{N-1}\\
&&&&a_{N-1}&b_{N-1}\end{pmatrix}-z\mathrm{Id},

(79)

where
\mathrm{Id}
is the identity matrix.

Then for
j<k

\displaystyle(J_{N}^{-1})_{j,k}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-j}a_{j}a_{j+1}\cdots a_{k-1}\frac{d_{k+1}\cdots d_{N}}{\delta_{j}\cdots\delta_{N}},

(80)

\displaystyle(J_{N}^{-1})_{j,j}

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{j+1}\cdots d_{N}}{\delta_{j}\cdots\delta_{N}},

(81)

where
d_{N}=b_{N-1}-z
and, for all
j=1,\cdots N-1
,
d_{j}
are solutions to the following difference equation

d_{j}=b_{j-1}-z-\frac{a_{j}^{2}}{d_{j+1}}.

(82)

We also have
\delta_{1}=b_{0}-z
and, for all
j=2,\cdots N
,
\delta_{j}
are solutions to the following difference equation

\delta_{j}=b_{j-1}-z-\frac{a_{j-1}^{2}}{\delta_{j-1}}.

(83)

Note that
J_{N}^{-1}
is symmetric and we have
(J_{N}^{-1})_{k,j}=(J_{N}^{-1})_{j,k}
. Hence, we have an expression for each entry.

In the following we will linearise both difference equations above and rewrite the inverse formula in different ways that are suitable for our further analysis.

Linearization of the difference equation I

Recursively define
\beta_{j}
by

\beta_{N}=a_{N-1},\quad\beta_{N-1}=b_{N-1}-z,\quad\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j-1}}=\frac{a_{j-1}}{d_{j}},\quad\text{for $j=1,\dots,N$}.

Hence,
\beta_{j}
is the solution to the following linear difference equation, by (
82
),

\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{j}\\
\beta_{j-1}\end{pmatrix}=A_{j}\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{j+1}\\
\beta_{j}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ where }A_{j}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-\frac{a_{j}}{a_{j-1}}&\left(b_{j-1}-z\right)\frac{1}{a_{j-1}}\end{pmatrix},

(84)

for
j=2,\dots,N-1
. Iteratively,

\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{j}\\
\beta_{j-1}\end{pmatrix}=A_{j}A_{j+1}\cdots A_{N-1}\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{N}\\
\beta_{N-1}\end{pmatrix}.

(85)

Let
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
N_{0}\leq N
. Take the ratio of
\left(J_{N}^{-1}\right)_{j,k}
and
\left(J_{N}^{-1}\right)_{j,N_{0}}
which are found by (
80
) or (
81
), and we rewrite the inverse formula for
j\leq N_{0}<k
to be

\left(J_{N}^{-1}\right)_{j,k}=(-1)^{k-N_{0}}\frac{\beta_{k}}{\beta_{N_{0}}}\left(J_{N}^{-1}\right)_{j,N_{0}}.

(86)

One purpose of the coming Section
5
is to show that in the case
b_{j}=b_{j,n}-x_{0}
,
a_{j}=a_{j,n}
,
z=\eta/n^{\alpha}
,
N=n+2mn^{\alpha/2+\varepsilon/3}
and
N_{0}=n-2mn^{\alpha/2+\varepsilon/3}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, given assumptions of Theorem
2.3
, we have
|\beta_{k}/\beta_{N_{0}}|\leq e^{-d_{2}(k-N_{0})n^{-\alpha/2}}
, for some constant
d_{2}>0
. The same is also true for the case
b_{j}=b_{j+n-2mn^{\alpha/2+\varepsilon/3},n}-x_{0}
,
a_{j}=a_{j+n-2mn^{\alpha/2+\varepsilon/3},n}
,
z=\eta/n^{\alpha}
,
N=4mn^{\alpha/2+\varepsilon/3}
and
N_{0}=2mn^{\alpha/2+\varepsilon/3}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, we have an estimate as in Proposition
4.1
, which is an analogue of the exponential part of (
78
).

To this end, the following quantities are essential in this estimate. The eigenvalues of
A_{j}
are

\omega_{j}^{+}\coloneqq\frac{b_{j-1}-z+\left(\left(b_{j-1}-z\right)^{2}-4a_{j}a_{j-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2a_{j-1}},\quad\omega_{j}^{-}\coloneqq\frac{b_{j-1}-z-\left(\left(b_{j-1}-z\right)^{2}-4a_{j}a_{j-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2a_{j-1}}.

(87)

The square root is taken with respect to the principal branch, so that
|\omega_{j}^{+}|>|\omega_{j}^{-}|
, whenever
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(b_{j-1}-z)>0
. Note that
A_{j}
can be diagonalised as

A_{j}=V_{j}\Omega_{j}V_{j}^{-1},\quad\text{where }V_{j}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
\omega_{j}^{+}&\omega_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega_{j}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{j}^{+}&0\\
0&\omega_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix}.

Also of importance is

M_{j}\coloneqq V_{j}^{-1}V_{j+1}-\mathrm{Id}=\frac{1}{\omega_{j}^{-}-\omega_{j}^{+}}\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{j}^{+}-\omega_{j+1}^{+}&\omega_{j}^{-}-\omega_{j+1}^{-}\\
\omega_{j+1}^{+}-\omega_{j}^{+}&\omega_{j+1}^{-}-\omega_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix},

(88)

where
\mathrm{Id}
is the identity matrix.

Note that Proposition
4.1
only matches the exponential part of (
78
). To match the
n^{\alpha/2}
factor of (
78
), we need the following ingredients as well.

Linearization of the difference equation II

Similarly, we recursively define
\gamma_{j}
by

\gamma_{1}=a_{1},\quad\gamma_{2}=b_{0}-z,\quad\frac{\gamma_{j}}{\gamma_{j+1}}=\frac{a_{j}}{\delta_{j}},\quad\text{for $j=1,\dots,N$}.

Then
\gamma_{j}
also admits a recursive formula by equation (
83
)

\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{j}\\
\gamma_{j+1}\end{pmatrix}=B_{j}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{j-1}\\
\gamma_{j}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ where }B_{j}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-\frac{a_{j-1}}{a_{j}}&\left(b_{j-1}-z\right)\frac{1}{a_{j}}\end{pmatrix},

(89)

for all
j=2,\dots,N-1
. The matrix
B_{j}
is called the transfer matrix for
\gamma_{j}
. Iteratively

\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{j}\\
\gamma_{j+1}\end{pmatrix}=B_{j}B_{j-1}\dots B_{2}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{1}\\
\gamma_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Choose any
a_{N}\neq 0
(e.g. One can pick
a_{N}=a_{N-1}
), rewrite (
80
) and (
81
) by
\beta_{j}
and
\gamma_{j}
, and we have another inverse formula for any
j\leq k

(J_{N}^{-1})_{j,k}=\frac{(-1)^{k-j}\gamma_{j}\beta_{k}}{\beta_{N}a_{N}\gamma_{N+1}}.

(90)

Note that the inverse formula is independent on the choice of
a_{N}
since
a_{N}\gamma_{N+1}=\gamma_{N}\delta_{N}
and
\gamma_{N}
and
\delta_{N}
are independent of
a_{N}
. Also note that
J_{N}
is symmetric, and hence we have an expression for all entries.

One purpose of the coming Section
6
is to show that in the case
b_{j}=b_{j+n-2mn^{\alpha/2+\varepsilon/3},n}-x_{0}
,
a_{j}=a_{j+n-2mn^{\alpha/2+\varepsilon/3},n}
,
z=\eta/n^{\alpha}
,
N=4mn^{\alpha/2+\varepsilon/3}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have precise asymptotics of
\beta_{k}/\beta_{N_{0}}
and
\gamma_{j}/\gamma_{N+1}
as
n\to\infty
whenever the assumptions of Theorem
2.3
are satisfied. Hence, we will have an asymptotic analogue to (
77
), cf. Proposition
4.3

To this end, the following quantities are essential in this estimate. The eigenvalues of
B_{j}
are

\lambda_{j}^{+}\coloneqq\frac{b_{j-1}-z+\left(\left(b_{j-1}-z\right)^{2}-4a_{j}a_{j-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2a_{j}},\quad\lambda_{j}^{-}\coloneqq\frac{b_{j-1}-z-\left(\left(b_{j-1}-z\right)^{2}-4a_{j}a_{j-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2a_{j}}.

(91)

The square root is taken with respect to the principal branch, such that
|\lambda_{j}^{+}|>|\lambda_{j}^{-}|
, whenever
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(b_{j-1}-z)>0
. Note that
B_{j}
can be diagonalised as

B_{j}=W_{j}\Lambda_{j}W_{j}^{-1},\quad\text{where }W_{j}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
\lambda_{j}^{+}&\lambda_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix},\quad\Lambda_{j}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{j}^{+}&0\\
0&\lambda_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix}.

Similarly we define
E_{j}
, which is an essential element for the further analysis, to be

E_{j}\coloneqq W_{j}^{-1}W_{j-1}-\mathrm{Id}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}^{-}-\lambda_{j}^{+}}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{j}^{+}-\lambda_{j-1}^{+}&\lambda_{j}^{-}-\lambda_{j-1}^{-}\\
\lambda_{j-1}^{+}-\lambda_{j}^{+}&\lambda_{j-1}^{-}-\lambda_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix}.

(92)

The identities (
86
) and (
90
) are the key ingredients in the proofs of Propositions
4.1
and
4.3
, respectively.

4
Proof of Theorem
2.3

In this section, we decompose the proof of Theorem
2.3
into a number of propositions, which we will prove in the upcoming sections, cf. Sections
5
,
6
, and
7
.

Our strategy is to first prove Theorem
2.3
for some special test functions as described in (
43
) , i.e.,

f(x)=\sum_{r=1}^{2M}c_{r}\frac{1}{x-\eta_{r}}

for some
\eta_{r}\in\{x+iy|x\in\mathbb{R},y\neq 0\}
, in Subsection
4.1
. Then, extend this result to compactly supported and continuously differentiable test functions in Subsection
4.2
.

4.1
Proof of Theorem
2.3
for Special Class of Test Functions

Recall that
\mathcal{J}
defined in (
32
) is the Jacobi matrix associated with an OPE and
f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\left(\mathcal{J}\right)
is defined as (
45
). We say
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
is near the edges if one of the conditions in (
24
) is satisfied.
Recall, from Section
3.3
, that the
m
-th cumulant with
m\geq 2
for the mesoscopic linear statistics
\mathcal{C}_{m}(X^{(n)}_{f,\alpha,x_{0}})
is given, via (
55
).
To show that the limiting fluctuations of
X^{(n)}_{f,\alpha,x_{0}}
are Gaussian, it is sufficient to show that the second cumulant converges to a positive number and all cumulants of order
m\geq 3
converge to zero, as indicated by Lemma
3.4
.

It turns out that the asymptotics of the
m
-th cumulant (
m\in\mathbb{N}
) only depends on the recurrence coefficients of order around
n
, i.e.,
a_{j,n}
,
b_{j,n}
for all
j\sim n
as
n\to\infty
. The precise window depends on the scale considered. This has been observed in various setups as in
[
7
,
8
,
16
]
. We will show that this is the case in our setup as well. To this end, we will truncate the (semi-infinite) Jacobi matrix
\mathcal{J}
into the block around the
n,n
-th entry. For technical reasons, we will conduct a two-step truncation. To be precise, let
\beta=\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
such that
0<\frac{\alpha}{2}<\beta<\frac{\alpha+1}{3}<1
. Let us define
J^{(r)}\coloneqq\mathcal{J}-x_{0}-\frac{\eta_{r}}{n^{\alpha}}
and further define

J_{n+2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\coloneqq P_{n+2mn^{\beta}}J^{(r)}P_{n+2mn^{\beta}}+Q_{n+2mn^{\beta}},\qquad F_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\coloneqq\sum_{r=1}^{2M}c_{r}\left(J_{n+2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\right)^{-1},

(93)

J_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\coloneqq P_{n+2mn^{\beta}}Q_{n-2mn^{\beta}}J^{(r)}Q_{n-2mn^{\beta}}P_{n+2mn^{\beta}}+P_{n-2mn^{\beta}}+Q_{n+2mn^{\beta}},

(94)

F_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}\coloneqq\sum_{r=1}^{2M}c_{r}\left(J_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\right)^{-1}.

(95)

The subscript
n\pm 2mn^{\beta}
emphasises that the relevant entries are those with indices ranging between
n-2mn^{\beta}
and
n+2mn^{\beta}
. The parameters
M
and
c_{r}
come from (
43
).

The following is our first result about these truncated matrices, which is an alternative to the Combes-Thomas estimates for
J_{n+2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}
and
J_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}
.

J^{(r)}_{n+2mn^{\beta}}=
Conditions
of
Theorem
2.3
n-2mn^{\beta}
n+2mn^{\beta}
\left(J^{(r)}_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\right)^{-1}=
n-(2m-1)n^{\beta}
n-(2m-1)n^{\beta}
n-2mn^{\beta}
n+2mn^{\beta}
Exponentially
Small
Exponentially
Small
J^{(r)}_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}=
Conditions
of
Theorem
2.3
P_{n-2mn^{\beta}}
0
0
n-2mn^{\beta}
n+2mn^{\beta}
\left(J^{(r)}_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}\right)^{-1}=
P_{n-2mn^{\beta}}
0
0
n+(2m-1)n^{\beta}
n+(2m-1)n^{\beta}
n+2mn^{\beta}
Exponentially
Small
Exponentially
Small

Figure 2
:
Illustration of Proposition
4.1
. Given the entries of
J^{(r)}_{n+2mn^{\beta}}
and
J^{(r)}_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}
with both indices ranging between
n-2mn^{\beta}
and
n+2mn^{\beta}
are well behaved, we have that the entries of
\left(J^{(r)}_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\right)^{-1}
and
\left(J^{(r)}_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}\right)^{-1}
are exponentially small in the shaded areas respectively. Note that the matrices are block matrices and we ignore the trivial blocks in the illustration.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\beta=\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
be such that
0<\frac{\alpha}{2}<\beta<\frac{\alpha+1}{3}<1
. Consider
\mathcal{J}
described above whose entries are
(a_{l,n},b_{l,n})_{l\geq 1}
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume conditions in Theorem
2.3
are satisfied. Consider
x_{0}
to be near the edges, i.e., (
24
). Then there exist constants
C_{0}>0
,
d_{0}>0

n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for any
n>n_{0}
,
j,k
with
|j-k|\geq n^{\beta}
and
\max\{j,k\}\geq n-(2m-1)n^{\beta}
,

\displaystyle\left|\left(\left(J_{n+2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\right)^{-1}\right)_{j,k}\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}n^{\alpha}e^{-d_{0}n^{\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}}},

(96)

\displaystyle\left|\left(\left(J_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\right)^{-1}\right)_{j,k}\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}n^{\alpha}e^{-d_{0}n^{\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}}}.

(97)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\left|\left(F_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\right)_{j,k}\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}n^{\alpha}e^{-d_{0}n^{\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}}},

(98)

\displaystyle\left|\left(F_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}\right)_{j,k}\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}n^{\alpha}e^{-d_{0}n^{\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}}}.

(99)

For an illustration see Figure
2
. For the proof of Proposition
4.1
, see Section
5.2
.

We note that the factor
n^{\alpha}
is not optimal in the estimates
\left(J_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\right)^{-1}
or
F_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}
. These are improved in Proposition
4.3
. However, this is enough for the next step, which is the following proposition lying in the core of the proof of Theorem
2.3
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\beta=\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
such that
0<\frac{\alpha}{2}<\beta<\frac{\alpha+1}{3}<1
. Let
f
and
M
as in (
42
). Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\geq 2
. Assume the conditions in Theorem
2.3
are satisfied and
x_{0}
is near the edges, i.e., (
24
). Then there exist constants
d^{\prime}>0
,
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
C_{m}^{\prime}>0
that only depend on
m
and
M
such that for all
n>n_{0}

\left|n^{\alpha m}\mathcal{C}_{m}(X^{(n)}_{f,\alpha,x_{0}})-\mathcal{C}_{m}^{(n)}\left(F_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}\right)\right|\leq C_{m}^{\prime}e^{-d^{\prime}n^{\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}}}.

(100)

We give the proof for
m=2
here. The proof for general
m
follows the same principles, but is rather technical and we postpone the details to Section
5.4
.

Proof of Propsition
4.2
.

Consider the case
m=2
.

The second cumulant is
\mathcal{C}_{2}(X^{(n)}_{f,\alpha,x_{0}})=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\left(\mathcal{J}\right)P_{n}f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\left(\mathcal{J}\right)P_{n}\right)-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}\left(\mathcal{J}\right)\right)^{(2)}P_{n}\right)
. Then by Lemma
3.5
we have

\left\|n^{\alpha}P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)-P_{n}F_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\right\|_{1}\leq\left|a_{n+2mn^{\beta},n}\right|\sum_{r=1}^{2M}\left\|P_{n}\left(J_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\right)^{-1}E_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\right\|_{1}\left\|B_{r,n}^{(0)}\right\|_{\infty}.

(101)

By (
98
) in Proposition
4.1
we conclude that the trace norm is exponentially small. Since the operator norm and
a_{n+2mn^{\beta},n}
is uniformly bounded as
n\to\infty
, we obtain that (
101
) is exponentially small. Similarly,

\left\|n^{2\alpha}P_{n}\left(f_{\alpha,x_{0}}^{(n)}(\mathcal{J})\right)^{(2)}-P_{n}F_{n+2mn^{\beta}}^{2}\right\|_{1}\leq\left|a_{n+2mn^{\beta},n}\right|\sum_{r=1}^{2M}n^{\alpha}\left\|P_{n}\left(J_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\right)^{-1}E_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\right\|_{1}\left\|B_{r,n}^{(1)}\right\|_{\infty}\\
+\left|a_{n+2mn^{\beta},n}\right|\sum_{r=1}^{2M}n^{\alpha}\left\|P_{n}F_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\left(J_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\right)^{-1}E_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\right\|_{1}\left\|B_{r,n}^{(0)}\right\|_{\infty}.

(102)

The first line on the right-hand side of (
102
) is exponentially small by the same argument as (
101
). Note that the trace norm of the second line on the right-hand side of (
102
) can be estimated to be a double sum

\left\|P_{n}F_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\left(J_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\right)^{-1}E_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\right\|_{1}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n+2mn^{\beta}}\left|\left(F_{n+2mn^{\beta}}\right)_{j,k}\left(\left(J_{n\pm 2mn^{\beta}}^{(r)}\right)^{-1}\right)_{k,n+2mn^{\beta}}\right|,

(103)

which is also exponentially small by (
98
) and (
99
) in Proposition
4.1
. Recall definition
\mathcal{C}_{m}^{(n)}
as (
64
). This shows that
\left|n^{m\alpha}\mathcal{C}_{m}(X^{(n)}_{f,\alpha,x_{0}})-\mathcal{C}_{m}^{(n)}(F_{n+2mn^{\beta}})\right|
is exponentially small for
m=2
.

Next, for any linear operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, we can write the difference of the cumulants by adding and subtracting extra terms to be

\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)}(\mathcal{A})-\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)}(\mathcal{B})=-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(\left(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}\right)Q_{n}\mathcal{A}P_{n}\right)-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(\mathcal{B}Q_{n}\left(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}\right)P_{n}\right).

(104)

Assume
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are symmetric (not necessarily Hermitian). Use the trace norm inequality
\|AB\|_{1}\leq\|A\|_{1}\|B\|_{\infty}
to obtain

\left|\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)}(\mathcal{A})-\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)}(\mathcal{B})\right|\leq\left\|P_{n}\left(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}\right)Q_{n}\right\|_{1}\left(\|\mathcal{A}\|_{\infty}+\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty}\right).

(105)

Then we take
\mathcal{A}=F_{n+2mn^{\beta}}
as (
